Register here . <p> If you prefer WhatsApp for updates , sign up to the BizNews channel here . <p> By Peter Dearlove <p> Under the driest of all dry places on earth there is a pool of ancient fresh water that supplies most of the daily needs of an entire nation and may well go on doing so for the next thousand years without replenishment . Now known as The Nubian Sandstone Aquifer System , it was only discovered by accident in 1953 during a Libyan Sahara Desert drilling program looking for oil . What they uncovered instead could be many times more valuable than oil , and hugely significant at a time when scientists are warning that the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The size , scope , history and future potential of the enormous underground resource make truly mind-boggling reading . The Aquifer lies below the Sahara Desert stretching across more than 2.5million square kilometres and spanning Chad , Libya , Sudan and most of Egypt . Its reserves are thought to be equivalent to 500 years of Nile River discharge and as much as 20 times as much as is held by the American Great Lakes . Thanks to a development called the Great Man Made River , it now supplies Libya with 70% of its freshwater needs ; 6.5 million cubic metres a day . All of it is described as ' fossil ' water , having been deposited during the last great ice age and never replenished since then . <p> The Great Man-Made River is a vast network of wells , drill holes and massive pipes running the length and breadth of the country and delivering water from the Aquifer to wherever it is needed , both for agriculture and human consumption . The project was drawn up nearly twenty years after the discovery of the aquifer , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ costing many billions of dollars , all funded by the Libyans themselves . The GMMR , for short , involved the excavation of nearly 100 million cubic metres of ground , and the manufacture and transport of 1,750 miles of giant pipes , the drilling of 1300 wells , many more than 500m deep . The water began to flow in 1991 , and it was soon dubbed ' the Eighth Wonder of the World ' . <p> Large and important as it is , the Nubian Aquifer is not the only underground water system attracting attention and interest . Sparked without doubt by the astonishing facts of the Nubian one , water experts and scientists have been looking for more . And the result has already been dramatic . In all there are thought to be nearly a hundred that could make truly significant contributions to the world 's freshwater needs . And there are others too that hold water that is only slightly saline but could be cheaply treated . Large ones of this particular kind have been found in Australia , China , North America and South @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ kilometres of treatable water . <p> Canada , the United States , Russia , Europe , Asia , and Latin America , also have their own underground resources . The Guarani Aquifer , widely spread below the countries of Argentina , Brazil , Paraguay and Uruguay , is one of the world 's largest , covering about 1.2m sq km and holding 40 000 cubic kilometres of fresh water . <p> In the US there is another of the truly great ones . The Ogallala Aquifer lies under eight central states and now provides water for massive irrigation projects as well as industrial needs . Increasing municipal use of Ogallala water has scientists worried that it could soon be depleted beyond recovery . <p> The Edwards Aquifer in Texas is a rare example of an underground water resource that is constantly being replenished . Despite providing the daily fresh water needs of nearly 2 million people , it is still full . Annual rainfall finds its way underground through a network of streams , rivers and lakes . <p> The planet has a great abundance of water but less than @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and can only be made fresh by expensive desalination. 70% is frozen at the Poles . Only 1% is readily available .. Of what we do have , agriculture uses roughly two thirds , and the rest we use to drink , and for cooking and washing . Unfortunately , however , even that meagre amount is under pressure from population growth , climate change , and in some places , poor water management . There is a distinct possibility , scientists say , that this trilogy could lead to serious water scarcity and shortages in many regions . A significant portion of the world 's population , over two billion people , already do not have access to enough safe drinking water . It is a growing global issue because the population explosion shows no sign of abating . <p> Of itself , climate change is more of a great disrupter than an actual threat to the planet 's quantum of fresh water . By altering weather patterns it can , and does , affect the availability of safe fresh drinking water . Severe droughts in some areas with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . A year of abundant rainfall may be followed by years of none , and vice versa . The unpredictability of such swings adds to the problem by making attempts at relief supply in emergency difficult and sometimes impossible . <p> The United Nations has been aware of and concerned by these issues for some time . In 2003 it established UN Water , specifically to keep a watchful eye on the one resource we can not live without , and to co-ordinate coherence and action on all issues affecting its safe supply . Some commentators believe there will soon be calls to add to the body 's responsibility with the creation of something like a ' water bank ' . Among its functions could well be to formalise and create an agenda for a co-ordinated effort at asset identification . Exactly how much do we have hidden away in our caverns underground ? Where , how much , how deep , what quality , and how can we make use it ? Some may baulk at the idea , others will see it as an important start towards a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @

( 123RF/PIPPOCARLOT ) <p> Sustainable finance has rapidly evolved from a niche concept to a fundamental component of the global financial landscape . As the impact of climate change and the degradation of nature become more pronounced , financial institutions are driving the strategic value in investments that not only promise returns but also contribute to environmental resilience . <p> This shift is n't just about managing risks , it 's about unlocking new opportunities to create sustainable value for investors and the planet . <p> Too often , finance is thought of primarily in terms of numbers on a balance sheet . But what if we flipped that narrative ? What if finance can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ replenishing soils and restoring wetlands while ensuring sustainable economic returns ? <p> Nedbank Corporate and Investment Banking ( CIB ) sees nature as an evolving asset class that holds substantial value for the planet and future generations of investors and communities alike . Research and development on the strategic alignment of financial systems with the protection , restoration and sustainable use of nature and biodiversity -- i.e. nature finance or natural capital -- are well underway . <p> Discussions on themes such as natural accounting are becoming more mainstream , with one local example being StatsSA 's 10-year Natural Capital Accounting Strategy to inform SA 's sustainable development policy objectives . <p> Internationally , the new Nature Restoration Regulation in the EU outlines a plan to enhance natural capital , and initiatives such as Science Based Targets for Nature and the Taskforce on Nature-related Financial Disclosures provide guidance to companies so they can better understand and report on nature . <p> In addition , the recent publication of Sustainable Bonds for Nature : A Practitioner 's Guide by the International Capital Market Association provides direction and recommendations for raising funding @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ SA , the stakes are high . Resilience in finance stretches beyond traditional boundaries and covers a variety of topics in the green and blue economies . <p> As an example , nature loss poses significant risks to the global economy ; many sectors including tourism , food consumption and energy are deeply dependent on healthy ecosystems . Financiers can help make protecting nature profitable through mainstreaming the conservation of nature as an objective in the financing markets and by structuring and offering commercially viable nature-based solutions . <p> In SA , we 've seen firsthand how natural ecosystems can act as a buffer against climate shocks . Nature opportunities are n't just about mitigating harm to nature ; they 're about actively restoring the world around us and doing so efficiently , sustainably and in harmony with nature . Doing so will not only benefit the climate and nature , but just as importantly protect our economies by providing critical additional avenues to support livelihoods for current and future generations . <p> There is growing recognition that nature-based solutions offer a pathway to climate resilience . Nedbank CIB has embraced this idea ; the bank understands that natural ecosystems hold the key for sustainable carbon sequestration and biodiversity protection as well as provide critical services underlying many of our basic needs . <p> Nedbank CIB believes finance can be a force for good -- a tool to improve livelihoods while positively impacting the natural systems that underpin life itself <p> Nature has therefore been identified as a strategic theme the bank can actively steward and embed within its sustainable finance activities , whether by mobilising capital towards eligible nature-positive outcomes or by integrating nature-based sustainability-linked @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> It 's clear that the era of sustainable finance is here to stay , but the question remains : what kind of future will it create ? @@130925596 <h> Designing inclusive African cities for climate resilience and social equity <p> Oct 31 , 2025 <p> Observed annually on 31 October , World Cities Day spotlights the transformative potential of people-centred smart cities in shaping equitable , prosperous , sustainable , and inclusive urban environments . This year 's theme underscores the imperative to design cities that enhance the quality of life and foster resilience for all communities . <p> In Africa , climate change intensifies the urgency of inclusive urban development . Without adequate response measures , an estimated 118-million people living in extreme poverty could face heightened exposure to drought , flooding , and extreme heat by 2030 . These climate-driven events threaten to damage infrastructure , disrupt economies , and compromise public health -- causing physical harm and mental distress -- as well as reduced access to essential commodities through disrupted supply chains and resource production . <p> Climate adaptation in Africa , therefore , means creating cities that resist and regenerate without reinforcing the social and economic inequities baked into many of Africa 's urban environments through the continent 's colonial history . Most of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the world are generally less well provided with services and amenities , have poorer air quality , and less access to green space -- factors with a range of knock-on effects for health and educational outcomes . Deprivation correlates with low life expectancy and poor life chances overall . <p> Africa 's urbanisation agenda presents a significant opportunity <p> Research suggests that nearly half of Africa 's population ( over 700-million people ) already live in urban areas , and this number is expected to double ( to 1,4-billion ) by 2050 as Africa sets an unprecedented record pace of urban expansion . <p> To meet growing demand , the temptation to build new cities on greenfield sites , away from existing city centres , is enormous . Yet , while rapid urban growth demands expanded urban space , overlooking the critical importance of retrofitting existing urban infrastructure risks undermining climate resilience and deepening the social inequalities that many African nations are working hard to address . <p> " Retrofitting serves a dual purpose , " explains Alison Groves , director of built ecology at WSP in Africa . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and integrates sustainable practices like water conservation , but it also revitalises underutilised or redundant structures -- such as converting outdated office blocks into housing . The opportunity to retrofit urban infrastructure to be smarter , more sustainable , and more inclusive is key to advancing climate resilience and social equity across Africa . " <p> By leveraging existing building stock to densify urban areas , cities can deliver affordable housing in proximity to established infrastructure , services , workplaces , and amenities -- accommodating more people in safer , more sustainable environments . <p> Groves adds : " It 's about meeting the challenge of growing people-centred smart cities without reinforcing existing social and economic divides , while ensuring environmental sustainability . We must consider our cities through the lens of the lived experience -- focusing on everything from environmental quality and walkability to connectivity , amenities , and local economic opportunity . " <p> Driving the imperative from inclusivity to prosperity <p> Transport infrastructure and planning are key enablers of trade and drivers of economic growth , making them critical considerations for ensuring African cities are not only @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ networks represent invaluable assets , not merely in terms of monetary worth but in the significant socioeconomic benefits they bring . The ability to move people , goods , and services seamlessly lies at the heart of modern life , connecting participants within the economy -- businesses and individuals -- and fostering progress . <p> " The impact of efficient and well-thought-out transport networks extends far beyond functionality , influencing societal transformation and environmental outcomes , " says John Rammutla , technical director ( discipline lead -- transportation and aviation ) at WSP . " Through thoughtful planning and collaboration , transport systems can become catalysts for improved quality of life and community development . " <p> In addition to more effective collaboration , leveraging Intelligent Transportation Systems ( ITS ) to digitalise transportation networks will further economic growth through efficiency and safety . ITS , then , constitutes an important part of the future African smart city as it integrates innovative information and communication technologies into transportation and traffic management to enhance safety , efficiency , and sustainability , while reducing congestion and enriching the driving experience . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lack reliable access to electricity , accounting for more than 80% of the global electricity access gap . At the same time , there is a strong push towards decarbonising Africa 's energy sectors . <p> According to the Africa Energy Report 2025 , Africa 's total power generation stands at over 980 terawatt hours ( TWh ) , and fossil fuels account for nearly 72% , while renewables hold a share of just over 27% . However , this reliance on fossil fuels is shifting , and rapidly . In 2023 , the money raised by African climate tech startups accounted for more than 30% of all funds raised by startups on the continent , placing climate tech second only to fintech . <p> Reshaping resilience through water resource management for future prosperity <p> Water considerations , too , must be integrated from the outset for smart , people-centred African cities , especially in water-scarce countries . Where the prevailing aging infrastructure in many African countries not only impacts reliable access to water , but water quality as well , and the expected largest wave of unprecedented urban growth @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Priya Moodley , technical director : water resources at WSP in Africa , says : " If African cities are to respond to this expected growth , and without exacerbating existing social and economic inequalities , physical infrastructure as well as protection and preservation practices must be adapted to meet people 's needs , sustainably . " <p> Moodley believes that all stakeholders in the value chain , from suppliers to regulators , must recognise the urgency of this challenge , understand where the greatest risks and opportunities can be found across the lifecycle , and work to develop and implement appropriate water strategies . <p> " To optimise resources , water management principles must guide the prioritisation of measures , starting by preventing unnecessary uses or losses of drinkable water , and promoting appropriate disposal of water that can not be reused without endangering human health or harming the environment . We take a holistic approach to planning and delivering projects for communities that are equitable , in balance with nature and future-ready , while always taking care to preserve what makes each one a unique place , " concludes Groves .

@@130925774 <p> With consumers globally reportedly spending between $25 and $100 a month on affordable everyday items , including apparel , from low-cost online retailers , it has meant more accessible clothing options for Africa 's growing middle class -- but has this sartorial excess been at the expense of the environment ? <p> The fast fashion online shopping experience is rapidly becoming a global norm . " I actually calculated how much I have spent since starting to use the app in 2020 , " Makgoe also admits . " It 's bad , because when I add up all of my orders , it 's about R15,000 ( $855 ) to R20,000 ( $1140 ) ... with each order maybe coming to about R3,000 ( $171 ) to R4,000 ( $228 ) . " <p> The brands are massively favored , but however their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ increasingly being globally deliberated by environmental experts . The fast-fashion brands are expanding , rapidly , across the globe with ultra-fast production cycles and mass , pr ? t- ? -porter lines . <p> Advertisement <p> As the world seeks sustainable solutions in everyday life , it 's part of a broader debate on fast fashion , slow fashion , and their lasting impacts . <p> Life In The Fast Lane <p> Liz Ricketts , the Founder and Executive Director of The Or Foundation in Ghana -- which works directly with fast fashion waste in the Kantamanto market in the country -- is concerned about the volume and speed with which fast fashion is dispensed by such business models and e-retailers worldwide . <p> Over the past two decades , the influx of clothing from the EU , U.S. , Canada , and China , has surged with the rise of fast fashion , while the value of clothing has steadily declined . <p> Advertisement <p> Environmental experts agree that this imbalance has fueled excessive waste and deepened the environmental crisis , placing even greater strain on the global fashion @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , excess is accepted as the norm but no country in the world has the waste management infrastructure to adequately manage textile waste . In fact , only 1% of clothing produced globally is recycled into new clothing , " Ricketts says . <p> " It comes as no surprise that Accra lacks recycling facilities and sufficient , engineered landfills , meaning much of this waste ends up in informal dumpsites , burned in open air or swept into gutters , ultimately ending up in the ocean . Our team removes a minimum of 20 tons of clothing and other plastic waste from the beach every single week . " <p> Co-Founder Abrima Erwiah adds that when clothing is made and sold cheaply , people buy quickly and discard quickly . <p> " We prefer not to single out individual companies , " she adds . " The challenge is the ultra-fast fashion model itself . It is built on speed , volume and synthetics , which accelerates extraction , pollution and waste at every stage of the value chain . <p> However , even major retailers pose a massive @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ moving to create more eco-friendly garments , it may not be enough , according to Rewoven Co-Founder , Esethu Cenga . <p> Advertisement <p> " The challenges we face because of fast fashion are quite dire , " Cenga says with a sigh , in an interview with FORBES AFRICA . " The growth of international fast fashion retailers , particularly in South Africa , has significant consequences . " <p> She adds : " When these low-priced products enter South Africa , local brands struggle to compete , impacting job creation and reducing opportunities for South African workers in the manufacturing sector . The issue is n't just about profits , it 's about the local fashion industry 's ability to sustain employment and create meaningful work . " <p> The biggest concern for Cenga is the waste generated by international fast fashion . She explains that brands that are n't present in Africa often send their waste to the continent under the guise of second-hand clothing donations . A large number of these garments are not durable , having been cheaply produced and designed with a short lifespan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's estimated that 50 million second-hand items arrive in Accra each week , generating around 160 tons of textile waste daily . The oceans are so polluted with discarded textiles that people have even reported finding pairs of jeans while swimming , " Cenga says . <p> Advertisement <p> " In South Africa , Customs SARS intercept much of this waste and incinerates it to prevent environmental damage , but this is n't a sustainable solution . While some of the second-hand clothing has led to a thriving resale market , much of it still hampers local fashion brands because imported , cheap clothing reduces demand for locally-produced products . " <p> Esethu Cenga Image by Rewoven <p> Ricketts reiterates this point , explaining that Ghana has imported over 147,000 metric tons of second-hand clothing in 2023 , and much of this passes through Kantamanto . <p> Fifteen million items of second-hand clothing ( called obroni w'awu or ' dead white man 's clothing ' locally ) pass through the market on a weekly basis , with 40% of that clothing ultimately going unsold and leaving the market as waste @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is different from what is immediately discarded as waste on arrival because it is so badly-stained or torn . <p> " This was n't always the case , " she adds , to FORBES AFRICA . " For decades , before fast fashion , this gamble paid off because enough of the garments inside the average bale were high-quality , durable , and easily resold . Such sales funded the repair or upcycling of the lower-quality items that would arrive in every bale . But today , the risk far outpaces the reward , with over half of the items in each bale having less value than the cost to manage the same items , driving retailers into debt and threatening a collapse of one of the largest reuse and upcycling economies in the world . " <p> Erwiah explains that " in Accra , large volumes of imported second-hand clothing regularly exceed what local markets and infrastructure can handle , especially when low-quality items arrive damaged or unsellable " . <p> " The result is pressure on livelihoods and public systems : bales that ca n't be sold become @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , when burned , polluting communities . The root cause sits upstream : global overproduction , overconsumption and the rapid churn of ultra-fast fashion . " <p> International Wear , Local Impact <p> In general , the fashion industry poses significant environmental challenges . According to Earth.org , it is a major driver of ecological strain due to its dependence on non-renewable resources , high greenhouse gas emissions , and intensive use of water and energy . In fact , it is the world 's second-largest consumer of water , requiring an estimated 700 gallons to produce a single cotton shirt and about 2,000 gallons to manufacture one pair of jeans . <p> The business model of most of the online retailers selling low-cost clothing involves producing a vast number of new designs weekly , encouraging consumers to buy more clothes than they need , and fueling , according to some experts , a culture of overconsumption . <p> This has also made it difficult for local designers , such as MAXHOSA AFRICA and Rewoven , who create unique products with a sustainable mindset . <p> " It 's a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up with , especially given the new challenge we 've been facing over the past three years -- counterfeits , " Laduma Ngxokolo , Founder and Creative Director of the South African knitwear brand MAXHOSA AFRICA , tells FORBES AFRICA . <p> " The main difference between us and fast fashion is that we cultivate original creative ideas and bring them to life through our manufacturing . " <p> Aja Barber , author of Consumed : The Need for Collective Change : Colonialism , Climate Change , and Consumerism , and a global voice critiquing fast fashion , says that the big problem with fast fashion and the impact it has on the continent also comes back to colonialism . <p> " Today 's legacy of colonialism looks just like the past . The raw resources and labor come from the Global South , the profit extracted from the Global North , " Barber tells FORBES AFRICA . " I would look at the fact that the fashion capitals of the world are all in the United States and Europe . Or the fact that the majority of the high-paid @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that many people can not name African designers off the top of their head and would never consider supporting African designers . " <p> On the concern around waste , she adds : " The idea that so much of the industry 's excess ends up in Africa ( Ghana , Kenya , Rwanda and Uganda ) is painfully unfair . And now , the industry has its sights set on various spots in Africa as manufacturing hubs but we know good and well how the industry treats its workers in the manufacturing sector . " <p> This then begs the question : Is slow fashion the sustainable solution ? <p> ... Not A Wasted Opportunity <p> The South African brand MAXHOSA AFRICA has built its ethos on the conviction that achieving zero waste is possible within this lifetime . <p> For its 2023 collection , the brand turned inward to embrace more sustainable practices , exemplified by the ' mosaic-like Bib Vest ' crafted entirely from off-cut materials -- a striking demonstration that environmental consciousness and luxury can coexist . According to Ngxokolo , MAXHOSA chooses to manufacture internally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ through the use of natural materials . The brand sources wool , mohair , and cotton from the Eastern Cape in South Africa , and incorporates viscose , a semi-natural fiber , to create garments that reflect its commitment to responsible production . <p> Aja Barber , author of Consumed ( Image Supplied ) <p> " It 's important for us because our brand is built on three pillars : Ubuntu , arts , and culture . Ubuntu is n't just about taking care of the people around you , but also the environment , " he says . " As much as we enjoy being alive today , we know we wo n't be here in the next 50 years -- and future generations deserve to enjoy the same fresh air , clean water , and sustainable , organic food that we have . " <p> In the city of Cape Town , three young people -- Tshepo Bhengu , Cenga and Lonwabo Mgoduso -- started Rewoven in 2018 when they started noticing that the big retail players were looking to sustainable materials for their garments . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ massive problem : textile waste . <p> " My business partner and I started visiting clothing manufacturers , literally knocking on their doors , asking , ' Do you have textile waste ? How much ? Ehat do you do with it ? ' " Cenga recalls . <p> " We were shocked to see how much production waste they generated daily . They admitted it was a massive problem -- they had to dump it every day and did n't know what else to do . That 's when we thought , ' Could textile waste be recycled the way plastic is , and could new products be made from it ? ' " <p> The first few years of Rewoven 's operations have been focused on understanding the landscape and determining what a feasible and scalable textile recycling model would look like . The next phase is to implement that model and scale it . <p> " Over the past five years , Rewoven has primarily operated as a collector and sorter , " Cenga says . " We have collected textile waste from factories , hospitals , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and sold it to fiber producers . These fibers are used in downcycled products like automotive carpet underlays and upholstery . " <p> Liz Ricketts , the Founder and Executive Director of The Or Foundation in Ghana ( Image Supplied ) <p> According to Stop Waste Colonialism -- a campaign by The OR Foundation in Ghana , which advocates for alternatives to the dominant fashion model -- there is no precise data on the size of the industry . However , available estimates suggest that between 80 billion and 150 billion new garments are produced every year . <p> " Unlike the carbon footprint for their products , production volume is a data point that is easy for brands to calculate . Unlike many other measurements of sustainability , production volume is a data point that is easy for the average consumer to understand , " the foundation states . <p> Ricketts has long-believed that overproduction is not an unintentional byproduct of fast fashion but rather a business model that has dominated the industry for years . And this model impacts everyone in the value chain . <p> " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lie in resale , circular living , and prioritizing quality over quantity -- is essential . But this shift alone will not adequately address the power imbalance or the overproduction , fueling waste systems in communities like Kantamanto . " <p> " Designers and textile organizations need to align themselves with industry bodies , because transparency will become the ultimate sophistication for any brand , " Ngxokolo adds . " With the growing demands for sustainability , many distributors already require certifications before they can carry your products -- things are tightening up . African designers need to be aware of this shift and join these organizations while there 's still time on their side . " <p> Cenga adds : " Textile recycling is still in its infancy , not just in South Africa , but globally . Even companies in capital-intensive countries with significant funding are struggling to do this . Rewoven aims to demonstrate that it 's possible to create a circular , sustainable textile system locally . " <p> Barber feels it needs to come down to the major retailers who mass-produce garments . " Corporations @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ extended producer responsibility on the clothing , " she says . " And some of that money should automatically go to places where the clothing waste impacts the local communities . Reparations must be a part of any recycling system . " 
@@130940647 <p> The Empire Partner Foundation , in partnership with TechFinancials News , is proud to announce Mahlatse Shaun Machethe , a young creative thinker with a passion for technology and storytelling , as the winner of the October TFS Q&amp;A competition <p> The Empire Partner Foundation &amp; TFS Q&amp;A Competition has officially wrapped up for the year with an inspiring final question that brought technology , youth innovation , and sustainability together : <p> " How can Generative AI help young farmers predict droughts , save water , and build climate-smart agriculture in South Africa ? " <p> The Empire Partner Foundation , in partnership with TechFinancials News , is proud to announce Mahlatse Shaun Machethe , a young creative thinker with a passion for technology and storytelling , as the winner of the October TFS Q&amp;A competition . Mahlatse delivered a visionary answer that captured the spirit of innovation and sustainability at the heart of Solve for X. <p> South Africa 's agricultural sector stands as a lifeline for rural communities and national food security . Yet , young farmers face mounting challenges from climate change , longer dry @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Machethe 's response showed how Generative AI can transform these challenges into opportunities . <p> The solution explained how Generative AI can predict droughts before they happen , using satellite data , weather models , and soil readings to create accurate local forecasts weeks or even months in advance . With early warnings delivered via mobile alerts , farmers can make informed choices about crop selection , irrigation schedules , and water storage . <p> For example , an AI system could notify Karoo farmers that rainfall is expected to be below average , advising them to reduce irrigation by 20% helping them conserve water without compromising yields . <p> Beyond forecasting , smart irrigation systems powered by AI can map out fields in real time , identifying exactly which areas need water and when . This precision can reduce water use by up to 40% , all while maintaining or even improving crop quality . <p> The technology also introduces " digital twins , " replicas of real farms that allow farmers to safely test new ideas , equipment , or planting strategies without the risk or cost @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tools , even farmers in the most remote areas can receive instant , local-language advice on soil health , pest control , and climate adaptation , all from their phones . <p> Building a Smarter , More Resilient Future <p> " In the next decade , I see AI becoming a core part of everyday farming in South Africa , " Machethe said . " AI tools should be accessible to every farmer , not just big commercial ones , helping them make data-driven decisions that increase productivity and protect the environment . " <p> By blending traditional knowledge with data-driven insights , young farmers are paving the way towards a resilient , climate-smart agricultural system <p> About the TFS &amp; EPF Q&amp;A Competition <p> This marks a powerful conclusion to the EPF &amp; TFS Q&amp;A Competition , a platform that has continuously encouraged young minds to think critically , act creatively , and design technology-driven solutions for a sustainable future . Each month , participants reimagine solutions to issues ranging from climate change and education to health and social impact , all aligned with the UN Sustainable Development Goals @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and support from EPF.
